[[Love|LOVE]]’S LABOUR’S LOST
THE first, 1598 edition of [[Love]]’s Labour’s Lost is the earliest play text to carry Shakespeare’s name on the title-page, which also refers to performance before the [[Queen]] ‘this last Christmas’. The play is said to be ‘Newly corrected and augmented’, so perhaps an earlier edition has failed to survive. Even so, the text shows every sign of having been printed from Shakespeare’s working papers, since it includes some passages in draft as [[Well|well]] as in revised form. We print the drafts as Additional Passages. The play was probably written some years before publication, in 1594 or 1595.
The setting is Navarre—a kingdom straddling the border between Spain and France—where the [[Young King|young King]] and three of his friends vow to devote the following three years to austere [[Self|self]]-improvement, forgoing the company of women. But they have forgotten the imminent arrival on a diplomatic mission of the [[Princess of France]] with, as it happens, three of her ladies; much [[Comedy|comedy]] derives from, first, the men’s embarrassed attempts to conceal from one another that they are falling in [[Love|love]], and second, the girls’ practical joke in exchanging identities when the men, disguised as Russians, come to entertain and to woo them. Shakespeare seems to have picked up the [[King]]’s friends’ names—Biron, [[Dumaine]], and [[Longueville]]—from leading figures in contemporary France, but to have invented the plot himself. He counterpoints the main action with events involving characters based in part on the type-figures of Italian commedia dell‘arte who reflect facets of the lords’ personalities. [[Costard]], an unsophisticated, open-hearted yokel, and his girl-friend Jaquenetta are sexually uninhibited; Don Adriano de Armado, ‘a refinèd traveller of Spain’ who also, though covertly, loves Jaquenetta, is full of pompous affectation; and Holofernes, a schoolmaster (seen always with his doting companion, the curate Sir Nathaniel), demonstrates the avid pedantry into which the young men’s verbal brilliance could degenerate. Much of the play’s language is highly sophisticated (this is, as the title-page claims, a ‘conceited [[Comedy|comedy]]’), in keeping with its subject matter. But the action reaches its climax when a messenger brings news which is communicated entirely without verbal statement. This is a theatrical masterstroke which also signals Shakespeare’s most daring experiment with comic form. ‘The scene begins to cloud’; in the play’s closing minutes the lords and ladies seek to readjust themselves to the new situation, and the play ends in subdued fashion with a third entertainment, the [[songs]] of the owl and the cuckoo.
[[Love]]’s Labour’s Lost was for long regarded as a play of excessive verbal sophistication, of interest mainly because of a series of supposed topical allusions; but a number of distinguished twentieth-century productions revealed its theatrical mastery.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
[[Ferdinand]], [[King|KING]] of Navarre
Don Adriano de ARMADO, an affected Spanish braggart
MOTE, his page

[[Princess of France|PRINCESS of France]]
[[Costard|COSTARD]], a Clown
JAQUENETTA, a country wench

Sir NATHANIEL, a curate
HOLOFERNES, a schoolmaster
Anthony DULL, a constable

MERCADE, a messenger

A FORESTER

[[Love]]’s Labour’s Lost

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Ferdinand]], [[King]] of Navarre, Biron, [[Longueville]], and [[Dumaine]] |
| [[King\|KING]] | Let fame, that all hunt after in their lives,<br>Live registered upon our brazen tombs,<br>And then grace us in the disgrace of [[Death\|death]]<br>When, spite of cormorant devouring [[Time\|time]],<br>Th’endeavour of this present breath may buy<br>That [[Honour\|honour]] which shall bate his scythe’s keen edge<br>And make us heirs of all [[Eternity\|eternity]].<br>Therefore, brave conquerors—for so you are,<br>That war against your own affections<br>And the huge army of the world’s desires—<br>Our late edict shall strongly stand in force.<br>Navarre shall be the [[Wonder\|wonder]] of the world.<br>Our [[Court\|court]] shall be a little academe,<br>Still and contemplative in living art.<br>You three—Biron, [[Dumaine]], and [[Longueville]]—<br>Have sworn for three years’ term to live with me<br>My fellow scholars, and to keep those statutes<br>That are recorded in this schedule here.<br>Your oaths are passed; and now subscribe your names,<br>That his own hand may strike his [[Honour\|honour]] down<br>That violates the smallest branch herein.<br>If you are armed to do as sworn to do,<br>Subscribe to your deep oaths, and keep it, too. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | I am resolved. ‘Tis but a three years’ fast.<br>The mind shall banquet, though the [[Body\|body]] pine.<br>Fat paunches have lean pates, and dainty bits<br>Make rich the ribs but bankrupt quite the wits.<br>He signs |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | My loving lord, [[Dumaine]] is mortified.<br>The grosser manner of these world’s delights<br>He throws upon the gross world’s baser slaves.<br>To [[Love\|love]], to wealth, to pomp I pine and die,<br>With all these living in [[philosophy]].<br>He signs |
| BIRON | I can but say their protestation over.<br>So much, dear liege, I have already sworn:<br>That is, to live and study here three years.<br>But there are other strict observances,<br>As not to see a woman in that term,<br>Which I [[Hope\|hope]] [[Well\|well]] is not enrolled there;<br>And one day in a week to touch no food,<br>And but one meal on every day beside,<br>The which I [[Hope\|hope]] is not enrolled there;<br>And then to [[Sleep\|sleep]] but three hours in the [[Night\|night]],<br>And not be seen to wink of all the day,<br>When I was wont to think no harm all [[Night\|night]],<br>And make a dark [[Night\|night]] too of half the day,<br>Which I [[Hope\|hope]] [[Well\|well]] is not enrolled there.<br>O, these are barren tasks, too hard to keep—<br>Not to see ladies, study, fast, not [[Sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Your oath is passed to pass away from these. |
| BIRON | Let me say no, my liege, an if you please.<br>I only swore to study with your grace,<br>And stay here in your [[Court\|court]], for three years’ space. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | You swore to that, Biron, and to the rest. |
| BIRON | By yea and nay, sir, then I swore in jest.<br>What is the end of study, let me know? |
| [[King\|KING]] | Why, that to know which else we should not know. |
| BIRON | Things hid and barred, you mean, from common sense. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Ay, that is study’s god-like recompense. |
| BIRON | Come on, then, I will swear to study so<br>To know the thing I am forbid to know,<br>As thus: to study where I [[Well\|well]] may dine<br>When I to feast expressly am forbid,<br>Or study where to meet some mistress fine<br>When mistresses from common sense are hid;<br>Or having sworn too hard a keeping oath,<br>Study to break it and not break my troth.<br>If study’s gain be thus, and this be so,<br>Study knows that which yet it doth not know.<br>Swear me to this, and I will ne’er say no. |
| [[King\|KING]] | These be the stops that hinder study quite,<br>And train our intellects to vain delight. |
| BIRON | Why, all delights are vain, but that most vain<br>Which, with [[Pain\|pain]] purchased, doth inherit [[Pain\|pain]];<br>As painfully to pore upon a book<br>To seek the light of [[Truth\|truth]] while [[Truth\|truth]] the while<br>Doth falsely blind the eyesight of his look.<br>Light, seeking light, doth light of light beguile;<br>So ere you find where light in darkness lies<br>Your light grows dark by losing of your eyes.<br>Study me how to please the eye indeed<br>By fixing it upon a fairer eye,<br>Who dazzling so, that eye shall be his heed,<br>And give him light that it was blinded by.<br>Study is like the heavens’ glorious sun,<br>That will not be deep searched with saucy looks.<br>Small have continual plodders ever won<br>Save base authority from others’ books.<br>These earthly godfathers of heaven’s lights,<br>That give a name to every fixed star,<br>Have no more profit of their shining nights<br>Than those that walk and wot not what they are.<br>Too much to know is to know naught but fame,<br>And every godfather can give a name. |
| [[King\|KING]] | How [[Well\|well]] he’s read, to reason against reading! |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | Proceeded [[Well\|well]], to stop all good proceeding. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | He weeds the corn and still lets grow the weeding. |
| BIRON | The [[Spring\|spring]] is near when green geese are a-breeding. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | How follows that? |
| BIRON | Fit in his place and [[Time\|time]]. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | In reason nothing. |
| BIRON | Something then in rhyme. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Biron is like an envious sneaping [[Frost\|frost]],<br>That bites the first-born infants of the [[Spring\|spring]]. |
| BIRON | [[Well]], say I am! Why should proud [[Summer\|summer]] boast<br>Before the [[Birds\|birds]] have any cause to sing?<br>Why should I joy in any abortive birth?<br>At Christmas I no more [[Desire\|desire]] a [[Rose\|rose]]<br>Than wish a [[Snow\|snow]] in May’s new-fangled shows,<br>But like of each thing that in season grows.<br>So you to study, now it is too late,<br>Climb o’er the [[house]] to unlock the little gate. |
| [[King\|KING]] | [[Well]], sit you out. Go [[Home\|home]], Biron. Adieu. |
| BIRON | No, my good lord, I have sworn to stay with you.<br>And though I have for barbarism spoke more<br>Than for that angel knowledge you can say,<br>Yet confident I’ll keep what I have sworn,<br>And bide the penance of each three years’ day.<br>Give me the paper. Let me read the same,<br>And to the strict’st decrees I’ll write my name. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (giving a paper)<br>How [[Well\|well]] this yielding rescues thee from shame! |
| BIRON | (reads) ‘Item: that no woman shall come within a mile of my [[Court\|court]].’ Hath this been proclaimed? |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Four days ago. |
| BIRON | Let’s see the penalty. ‘On [[Pain\|pain]] of losing her tongue.’ Who devised this penalty? |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Marry, that did I. |
| BIRON | Sweet lord, and why? |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | To fright them hence with that dread penalty. |
| BIRON | A dangerous law against gentility.<br>‘Item: if any man be seen to talk with a woman within<br>the term of three years, he shall endure such public<br>shame as the rest of the [[Court\|court]] can possible devise.’<br>This article, my liege, yourself must break;<br>For [[Well\|well]] you know here comes in embassy<br>The French [[King]]’s daughter with yourself to speak—<br>A maid of grace and complete majesty—<br>About surrender-up of Aquitaine<br>To her decrepit, sick, and bedrid father.<br>Therefore this article is made in vain,<br>Or vainly comes th’admirèd Princess hither. |
| [[King\|KING]] | What say you, lords? Why, this was quite forgot. |
| BIRON | So study evermore is overshot.<br>While it doth study to have what it would,<br>It doth forget to do the thing it should;<br>And when it hath the thing it hunteth most,<br>’Tis won as towns with [[Fire\|fire]]—so won, so lost. |
| [[King\|KING]] | We must of force dispense with this decree.<br>She must lie here, on mere necessity. |
| BIRON | Necessity will make us all forsworn<br>Three thousand times within this three years’ space;<br>For every man with his affects is born,<br>Not by might mastered, but by special grace.<br>If I break [[Faith\|faith]], this word shall speak for me:<br>I am forsworn on mere necessity.<br>So to the laws at large I write my name,<br>And he that breaks them in the least degree<br>Stands in attainder of eternal shame.<br>He signs<br>Suggestions are to other as to me,<br>But I believe, although I seem so loath,<br>I am the last that will last keep his oath.<br>But is there no quick recreation granted? |
| [[King\|KING]] | Ay, that there is. Our [[Court\|court]], you know, is haunted<br>With a refined traveller of Spain,<br>A man in all the world’s new fashion planted,<br>That hath a mint of phrases in his brain.<br>One who the music of his own vain tongue<br>Doth ravish like enchanting harmony;<br>A man of complements, whom right and wrong<br>Have chose as umpire of their mutiny.<br>This [[Child\|child]] of fancy, that Armado hight,<br>For interim to our studies shall relate<br>In high-borne words the worth of many a knight<br>From tawny Spain lost in the world’s [[debate]].<br>How you delight, my lords, I know not, I;<br>But I protest I [[Love\|love]] to hear him lie,<br>And I will use him for my minstrelsy. |
| BIRON | Armado is a most illustrious wight,<br>A man of [[Fire\|fire]]-new words, fashion’s own knight. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | [[Costard]] the swain and he shall be our sport,<br>And so to study three years is but short. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter a constable, Anthony Dull, with [[Costard]] with a letter |
| DULL | Which is the Duke’s own person? |
| BIRON | This, fellow. What wouldst? |
| DULL | I myself reprehend his own person, for I am his grace’s farborough. But I would see his own person in flesh and [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| BIRON | This is he. |
| DULL | Senor Arm—Arm—commends you. There’s villainy abroad. This letter will tell you more. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Sir, the contempts thereof are as touching me. [[King\|KING]] A letter from the magnificent Armado. |
| BIRON | How low soever the matter, I [[Hope\|hope]] in God for high words. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | A high [[Hope\|hope]] for a low heaven. God grant us patience. |
| BIRON | To hear, or forbear laughing? |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | To hear meekly, sir, and to laugh moderately, or to forbear both. |
| BIRON | [[Well]], sir, be it as the style shall give us cause to climb in the merriness. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | The matter is to me, sir, as concerning Jaquenetta. The manner of it is, I was taken with the manner. |
| BIRON | In what manner? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | In manner and form following, sir—all those three. I was seen with her in the manor [[house]], sitting with her upon the form, and taken following her into the park; which put together is ‘in manner and form following’. Now, sir, for the manner: it is the manner of a man to speak to a woman. For the form: in some form. |
| BIRON | For the ‘following’, sir? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | As it shall follow in my correction; and God defend the right. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Will you hear this letter with attention? |
| BIRON | As we would hear an oracle. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Such is the simplicity of man to hearken after the flesh. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (reads) ‘Great deputy, the welkin’s vicegerent and sole dominator of Navarre, my [[Soul\|soul]]’s earth’s god, and [[Body\|body]]’s fostering patron’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Not a word of [[Costard]] yet. |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘So it is’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | It may be so; but if he say it is so, he is, in telling true, but so. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Peace! |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Be to me and every man that dares not fight. |
| [[King\|KING]] | No words! |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Of other men’s [[Secrets\|secrets]], I beseech you. |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘So it is, besieged with sable-coloured [[Melancholy\|melancholy]], I did commend the black-oppressing [[Humour\|humour]] to the most wholesome physic of thy health-giving air, and, as I am a gentleman, betook myself to walk. The [[Time\|time]] when? About the sixth hour, when beasts most graze, [[Birds\|birds]] best peck, and men sit down to that nourishment which is called supper. So much for the [[Time\|time]] when. Now for the ground which—which, I mean, I walked upon. It is yclept thy park. Then for the place where—where, I mean, I did encounter that obscene and most preposterous event that draweth from my [[Snow\|snow]]-white pen the ebon-coloured ink which here thou viewest, beholdest, surveyest, or seest. But to the place where. It standeth north-north-east and by east from the west corner of thy curious-knotted [[Garden\|garden]]. There did I see that low-spirited swain, that base minnow of thy mirth’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Me? |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘That unlettered, small-knowing sout’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Me? |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘That shallow vassal’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Still me? |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘Which, as I remember, hight [[Costard]]’— |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | O, me! |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘Sorted and consorted, contrary to thy established proclaimed edict and continent canon, with, with, O with—but with this I passion to say wherewith’—[[Costard\|COSTARD]] With a wench. |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘With a [[Child\|child]] of our grandmother Eve, a female, or for thy more sweet understanding a woman. Him I, as my ever-esteemed duty pricks me on, have sent to thee, to receive the meed of punishment, by thy sweet grace’s officer Anthony Dull, a man of good repute, carriage, bearing, and estimation.’ |
| DULL | Me, an’t shall please you. I am Anthony Dull. |
| [[King\|KING]] | ‘For Jaquenetta—so is the weaker vessel called—which I apprehended with the aforesaid swain, I keep her as a vessel of thy law’s fury, and shall at the least of thy sweet notice bring her to trial. Thine in all compliments of devoted and heartburning heat of duty,<br>Don Adriano de Armado.’ |
| BIRON | This is not so [[Well\|well]] as I looked for, but the best that ever I heard. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Ay, the best for the worst. (To [[Costard]]) But, sirrah, what say you to this? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Sir, I confess the wench. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Did you hear the proclamation? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I do confess much of the hearing it, but little of the marking of it. |
| [[King\|KING]] | It was proclaimed a year’s [[Imprisonment\|imprisonment]] to be taken with a wench. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I was taken with none, sir. I was taken with a [[Damsel\|damsel]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] | [[Well]], it was proclaimed ‘[[Damsel\|damsel]]’. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | This was no [[Damsel\|damsel]], neither, sir. She was a virgin. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[King\|KING]] It is so varied, too, for it was proclaimed ‘virgin’. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | If it were, I deny her virginity. I was taken with a maid. |
| [[King\|KING]] | This ‘maid’ will not serve your turn, sir. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | This maid will serve my turn, sir. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Sir, I will pronounce your sentence. You shall fast a week with bran and [[Water\|water]]. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I had rather pray a month with mutton and porridge. |
| [[King\|KING]] | And [[Don Armado]] shall be your keeper.<br>My lord Biron, see him delivered o’er,<br>And go we, lords, to put in practice that<br>Which each to other hath so strongly sworn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt the [[King]], [[Longueville]], and [[Dumaine]] |
| BIRON | I’ll lay my head to any good man’s hat<br>These oaths and laws will prove an idle scorn.<br>Sirrah, come on. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I suffer for the [[Truth\|truth]], sir; for true it is I was taken with Jaquenetta, and Jaquenetta is a true girl, and therefore, welcome the sour cup of prosperity, affliction may one day smile again; and till then, sit thee down, sorrow. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado and Mote, his page |
| ARMADO | Boy, what sign is it when a man of great spirit grows [[Melancholy\|melancholy]]? |
| MOTE | A great sign, sir, that he will look sad. |
| ARMADO | Why, sadness is one and the selfsame thing, dear imp. |
| MOTE | No, no, O Lord, sir, no. |
| ARMADO | How canst thou part sadness and [[Melancholy\|melancholy]], my tender juvenal? |
| MOTE | By a familiar demonstration of the working, my tough señor. |
| ARMADO | Why ‘tough señor’? Why ‘tough señor’? |
| MOTE | Why ‘tender juvenal’? Why ‘tender juvenal’? |
| ARMADO | I spoke it, tender juvenal, as a congruent epitheton appertaining to thy young days, which we may nominate ‘tender’. |
| MOTE | And I, tough señor, as an appertinent title to your old [[Time\|time]], which we may name ‘tough’. |
| ARMADO | Pretty and apt. |
| MOTE | How mean you, sir? I ‘pretty’ and my saying ‘apt’? Or I ‘apt’ and my saying ‘pretty’? |
| ARMADO | Thou ‘pretty’, because little. |
| MOTE | Little pretty, because little. Wherefore ‘apt’? |
| ARMADO | And therefore ‘apt’ because quick. |
| MOTE | Speak you this in my praise, master? |
| ARMADO | In thy condign praise. |
| MOTE | I will praise an eel with the same praise. |
| ARMADO | What—that an eel is ingenious? |
| MOTE | That an eel is quick. |
| ARMADO | I do say thou art quick in answers. Thou heatest my [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| MOTE | I am answered, sir. |
| ARMADO | I [[Love\|love]] not to be crossed. |
| MOTE | (aside) He speaks the mere contrary-crosses [[Love\|love]] not him. |
| ARMADO | I have promised to study three years with the Duke. |
| MOTE | You may do it in an hour, sir. |
| ARMADO | Impossible. - MOTE How many is one, thrice told? |
| ARMADO | I am ill at reckoning; it fitteth the spirit of a tapster. |
| MOTE | You are a gentleman and a gamester, sir. ARMADO I confess both. They are both the varnish of a complete man. |
| MOTE | Then I am sure you know how much the gross sum of deuce-ace amounts to. |
| ARMADO | It doth amount to one more than two. |
| MOTE | Which the base vulgar do call three. |
| ARMADO | True. |
| MOTE | Why, sir, is this such a piece of study ? Now here is ‘three’ studied ere ye’ll thrice wink, and how easy it is to put ‘years’ to the word ‘three’ and study ‘three years’ in two words, the dancing [[Horse\|horse]] will tell you. ARMADO A most fine figure. |
| MOTE | (aside) To prove you a cipher. |
| ARMADO | I will hereupon confess I am in [[Love\|love]]; and as it is base for a soldier to [[Love\|love]], so am I in [[Love\|love]] with a base wench. If drawing my [[Sword\|sword]] against the [[Humour\|humour]] of affection would deliver me from the reprobate thought of it, I would take [[Desire\|desire]] prisoner and ransom him to any French courtier for a new-devised curtsy. I think scorn to sigh. Methinks I should outswear Cupid. Comfort me, boy. What great men have been in [[Love\|love]]? |
| MOTE | Hercules, master. |
| ARMADO | Most sweet Hercules! More authority, dear boy. Name more—and, sweet my [[Child\|child]], let them be men of good repute and carriage. |
| MOTE | Samson, master; he was a man of good carriage, great carriage, for he carried the town-gates on his back like a porter, and he was in [[Love\|love]]. |
| ARMADO | O [[Well\|well]]-knit Samson, strong-jointed Samson! I do excel thee in my rapier as much as thou didst me in carrying gates. I am in [[Love\|love]], too. Who was Samson’s [[Love\|love]], my dear Mote? |
| MOTE | A woman, master. |
| ARMADO | Of what complexion ? |
| MOTE | Of all the four, or the three, or the two, or one of the four. |
| ARMADO | Tell me precisely of what complexion? |
| MOTE | Of the sea-[[Water\|water]] green, sir. |
| ARMADO | Is that one of the four complexions? |
| MOTE | As I have read, sir; and the best of them, too. |
| ARMADO | Green indeed is the colour of [[lovers]], but to have a [[Love\|love]] of that colour, methinks Samson had small reason for it. He surely affected her for her wit. |
| MOTE | It was so, sir, for she had a green wit. |
| ARMADO | My [[Love\|love]] is most immaculate [[White and Red\|white and red]]. |
| MOTE | Most maculate thoughts, master, are masked under such colours. |
| ARMADO | Define, define, [[Well\|well]]-educated infant. |
| MOTE | My father’s wit and my mother’s tongue assist me! |
| ARMADO | Sweet invocation of a [[Child\|child]]!—most pretty and pathetical. |
| MOTE | If she be made of [[White and Red\|white and red]]<br>Her faults will ne’er be known,<br>For blushing cheeks by faults are bred<br>And fears by pale white shown.<br>Then if she [[Fear\|fear]] or be to blame,<br>By this you shall not know;<br>For still her cheeks possess the same<br>Which native she doth owe.<br>A dangerous rhyme, master, against the reason of [[White and Red\|white and red]]. |
| ARMADO | Is there not a ballad, boy, of the [[King]] and the Beggar? |
| MOTE | The world was very guilty of such a ballad some three ages since, but I think now ’tis not to be found; or if it were, it would neither serve for the writing nor the tune. |
| ARMADO | I will have that subject newly writ o’er, that I may example my digression by some mighty precedent. Boy, I do [[Love\|love]] that country girl that I took in the park with the rational hind [[Costard]]. She deserves [[Well\|well]]. |
| MOTE | (aside) To be whipped—and yet a better [[Love\|love]] than my master. |
| ARMADO | Sing, boy. My spirit grows heavy in [[Love\|love]]. |
| MOTE | And that’s great marvel, loving a light wench. ARMADO I say, sing. |
| MOTE | Forbear till this company be past. Enter [[Costard]] the clown, Constable Dull, and Jaquenetta, a wench |
| DULL | (to Armado) Sir, the Duke’s pleasure is that you keep [[Costard]] safe, and you must suffer him to take no delight, nor no penance, but a must fast three days a week. For this [[Damsel\|damsel]], I must keep her at the park. She is allowed for the dey-woman. Fare you [[Well\|well]]. |
| ARMADO | (aside) I do betray myself with blushing.—Maid. |
| JAQUENETTA | Man. |
| ARMADO | I will visit thee at the lodge. |
| JAQUENETTA | That’s hereby. |
| ARMADO | I know where it is situate. |
| JAQUENETTA | Lord, how wise you are! |
| ARMADO | I will tell thee wonders. |
| JAQUENETTA | With that face? |
| ARMADO | I [[Love\|love]] thee. |
| JAQUENETTA | So I heard you say. |
| ARMADO | And so farewell. |
| JAQUENETTA | Fair weather after you. |
| *(didascalia)* | DULL Come, Jaquenetta, away. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Dull and Jaquenetta |
| ARMADO | [[Villain]], thou shalt fast for thy offences ere thou be pardoned. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | [[Well]], sir, I [[Hope\|hope]] when I do it I shall do it on a full stomach. |
| ARMADO | Thou shalt be heavily punished. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I am more bound to you than your fellows, for they are but lightly rewarded. |
| ARMADO | Take away this [[Villain\|villain]]. Shut him up. |
| MOTE | Come, you transgressing slave. Away! |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Let me not be pent up, sir. I will fast, being loose. |
| MOTE | No, sir. That were fast and loose. Thou shalt to [[Prison\|prison]]. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | [[Well]], if ever I do see the merry days of desolation that I have seen, some shall see. |
| MOTE | What shall some see? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Nay, nothing, Master Mote, but what they look upon. It is not for prisoners to be too silent in their words, and therefore I will say nothing. I thank God I have as little patience as another man, and therefore I can be quiet. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Mote and [[Costard]] |
| ARMADO | I do affect the very ground—which is base-where her shoe—which is baser—guided by her foot—which is basest—doth tread. I shall be forsworn—which is a great argument of falsehood-if I [[Love\|love]]. And how can that be true [[Love\|love]] which is falsely attempted? [[Love]] is a familiar; [[Love\|love]] is a devil. There is no evil angel but [[Love\|love]]. Yet was Samson so tempted, and he had an excellent strength. Yet was Solomon so seduced, and he had a very good wit. Cupid’s butt-shaft is too hard for Hercules’ club, and therefore too much odds for a Spaniard’s rapier. The first and second cause will not serve my turn: the passado he respects not, the duello he regards not. His disgrace is to be called boy, but his glory is to subdue men. Adieu, valour; rust, rapier; be still, drum: for your manager is in [[Love\|love]]; yea, he loveth. Assist me, some extemporal god of rhyme, for I am sure I shall turn [[Sonnet\|sonnet]]. Devise wit, write pen, for I am for whole volumes, in folio. Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the [[Princess of France]] with three attending ladies-[[Maria]], Catherine, and [[Rosaline]]-and three lords, one named Boyet |
| BOYET | Now, madam, summon up your dearest spirits.<br>Consider who the [[King]] your father sends,<br>To whom he sends, and what’s his embassy:<br>Yourself, held precious in the world’s esteem,<br>To parley with the sole inheritor<br>Of all perfections that a man may owe,<br>Matchless Navarre; the plea of no less weight<br>Than Aquitaine, a dowry for a [[Queen\|queen]].<br>Be now as prodigal of all dear grace<br>As [[Nature\|nature]] was in making graces dear<br>When she did starve the general world beside<br>And prodigally gave them all to you. |
| PRINCESS | Good Lord Boyet, my [[Beauty\|beauty]], though but mean,<br>Needs not the painted flourish of your praise.<br>[[Beauty]] is bought by judgement of the eye,<br>Not uttered by base sale of chapmen’s tongues.<br>I am less proud to hear you tell my worth<br>Than you much willing to be counted wise<br>In spending your wit in the praise of mine.<br>But now to task the tasker: good Boyet,<br>You are not ignorant all-telling fame<br>Doth noise abroad Navarre hath made a vow<br>Till painful study shall outwear three years<br>No woman may approach his silent [[Court\|court]].<br>Therefore to’s seemeth it a needful course,<br>Before we enter his forbidden gates,<br>To know his pleasure; and in that behalf,<br>Bold of your worthiness, we single you<br>As our best-moving fair solicitor.<br>Tell him the daughter of the [[King of France]]<br>On serious business, craving quick dispatch,<br>Importunes personal conference with his grace.<br>Haste, signify so much while we attend,<br>Like humble-visaged suitors, his high will. |
| BOYET | Proud of employment, willingly I go. |
| PRINCESS | All [[Pride\|pride]] is willing [[Pride\|pride]], and yours is so. Exit Boyet<br>Who are the votaries, my loving lords,<br>That are vow-fellows with this virtuous duke?<br>A LORD<br>Lord [[Longueville]] is one. |
| PRINCESS | Know you the man? |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | I know him, madam. At a [[Marriage\|marriage]] feast<br>Between Lord Perigord and the beauteous heir<br>Of [[Jaques]] Fauconbridge solemnized<br>In Normandy saw I this [[Longueville]].<br>A man of sovereign parts he is esteemed,<br>[[Well]] fitted in arts, glorious in arms.<br>Nothing becomes him ill that he would [[Well\|well]].<br>The only soil of his fair virtue’s gloss-<br>If virtue’s gloss will stain with any soil-<br>Is a sharp wit matched with too blunt a will,<br>Whose edge hath power to cut, whose will still wills<br>It should none spare that come within his power. |
| PRINCESS | Some merry mocking lord, belike-is’t so? |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | They say so most that most his humours know. |
| PRINCESS | Such short-lived wits do wither as they grow.<br>Who are the rest? |
| CATHERINE | The young [[Dumaine]], a [[Well\|well]]-accomplished youth,<br>Of all that virtue [[Love\|love]] for virtue loved.<br>Most power to do most harm, least knowing ill,<br>For he hath wit to make an ill shape good,<br>And shape to win grace, though he had no wit.<br>I saw him at the Duke Alençon’s once,<br>And much too little of that good I saw<br>Is my report to his great worthiness. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Another of these students at that [[Time\|time]]<br>Was there with him, if I have heard a [[Truth\|truth]].<br>Biron they call him, but a merrier man, |
| *(didascalia)* | Within the limit of becoming mirth, |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | I never spent an hour’s talk withal.<br>His eye begets occasion for his wit,<br>For every object that the one doth catch<br>The other turns to a mirth-moving jest,<br>Which his fair tongue, conceit’s expositor,<br>Delivers in such apt and gracious words<br>That aged ears play truant at his tales,<br>And younger hearings are quite ravished,<br>So sweet and voluble is his discourse. |
| PRINCESS | God bless my ladies, are they all in [[Love\|love]],<br>That every one her own hath garnished<br>With such bedecking ornaments of praise?<br>A LORD<br>Here comes Boyet. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Boyet |
| PRINCESS | Now, what admittance, lord? |
| BOYET | Navarre had notice of your fair approach,<br>And he and his competitors in oath<br>Were all addressed to meet you, gentle lady,<br>Before I came. Marry, thus much I have learnt:<br>He rather means to lodge you in the field,<br>Like one that comes here to besiege his [[Court\|court]],<br>Than seek a dispensation for his oath<br>To let you enter his unpeopled [[house]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Navarre, [[Longueville]], [[Dumaine]], and Biron |
| BOYET | Here comes Navarre. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Fair Princess, welcome to the [[Court\|court]] of Navarre. |
| PRINCESS | ‘Fair’ I give you back again, and welcome I have not yet. The roof of this [[Court\|court]] is too high to be yours, and welcome to the wide fields too base to be mine. |
| [[King\|KING]] | You shall be welcome, madam, to my [[Court\|court]]. |
| PRINCESS | I will be welcome, then. Conduct me thither. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Hear me, dear lady. I have sworn an oath— |
| PRINCESS | Our Lady help my lord He’ll be forsworn. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Not for the world, fair madam, by my will. |
| PRINCESS | Why, will shall break it—will and nothing else. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Your ladyship is ignorant what it is. |
| PRINCESS | Were my lord so his ignorance were wise,<br>Where now his knowledge must prove ignorance.<br>I hear your grace hath sworn out housekeeping.<br>’Tis deadly sin to keep that oath, my lord,<br>And sin to break it.<br>But pardon me, I am too sudden-bold.<br>To teach a teacher ill beseemeth me.<br>Vouchsafe to read the purpose of my coming,<br>And suddenly resolve me in my suit.<br>She gives him a paper |
| [[King\|KING]] | Madam, I will, if suddenly I may. |
| PRINCESS | You will the sooner that I were away,<br>For you’ll prove perjured if you make me stay.<br>Navarre reads the paper |
| BIRON | (to [[Rosaline]])<br>Did not I [[Dance\|dance]] with you in Brabant once? |
| *(didascalia)* | FROSALINE |
| BIRON | Did not I [[Dance\|dance]] with you in Brabant once? |
| BIRON | I know you did. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | How needless was it then<br>To ask the question! |
| BIRON | You must not be so quick. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | ‘Tis ’long of you, that spur me with such questions. |
| BIRON | Your wit’s too hot, it speeds too fast, ’twill tire. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | Not till it leave the rider in the mire. |
| BIRON | What [[Time\|time]] o’ day? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | The hour that fools should ask. |
| BIRON | Now fair befall your mask. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | Fair fall the face it covers. |
| BIRON | And send you many [[lovers]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BIRON | Amen, so you be none. |
| BIRON | Nay, then will I be gone. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (to the Princess)<br>Madam, your father here doth intimate<br>The payment of a hundred thousand crowns,<br>Being but the one-half of an entire sum<br>Disbursed by my father in his wars.<br>But say that he or we—as neither have—<br>Received that sum, yet there remains unpaid<br>A hundred thousand more, in surety of the which<br>One part of Aquitaine is bound to us,<br>Although not valued to the money’s worth.<br>If then the [[King]] your father will restore<br>But that one half which is unsatisfied,<br>We will give up our right in Aquitaine<br>And hold fair [[Friendship\|friendship]] with his majesty.<br>But that, it seems, he little purposeth,<br>For here he doth demand to have repaid<br>A hundred thousand crowns, and not demands,<br>On payment of a hundred thousand crowns,<br>To have his title live in Aquitaine,<br>Which we much rather had depart withal,<br>And have the money by our father lent,<br>Than Aquitaine, so gelded as it is.<br>Dear Princess, were not his requests so far<br>From reason’s yielding, your fair [[Self\|self]] should make<br>A yielding ’gainst some reason in my breast,<br>And go [[Well\|well]] satisfied to France again. |
| PRINCESS | You do the [[King]] my father too much wrong,<br>And wrong the reputation of your name,<br>In so unseeming to confess receipt<br>Of that which hath so faithfully been paid. |
| [[King\|KING]] | I do protest I never heard of it,<br>And if you prove it I’ll repay it back<br>Or yield up Aquitaine. |
| PRINCESS | We arrest your word.<br>Boyet, you can produce acquittances<br>For such a sum from special officers<br>Of Charles, his father. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Satisfy me so. |
| BOYET | So please your grace, the packet is not come<br>Where that and other specialties are bound.<br>Tomorrow you shall have a sight of them. |
| [[King\|KING]] | It shall suffice me, at which interview<br>All liberal reason I will yield unto.<br>Meantime receive such welcome at my hand<br>As [[Honour\|honour]], without breach of [[Honour\|honour]], may<br>Make tender of to thy true worthiness.<br>You may not come, fair princess, within my gates,<br>But here without you shall be so received<br>As you shall deem yourself lodged in my heart,<br>Though so denied fair harbour in my [[house]].<br>Your own good thoughts excuse me, and farewell.<br>Tomorrow shall we visit you again. |
| PRINCESS | Sweet health and fair desires consort your grace. |
| [[King\|KING]] | Thy own wish wish I thee in every place. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit with [[Longueville]] and [[Dumaine]] |
| BIRON | (to [[Rosaline]]) Lady, I will commend you to mine own heart. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Pray you, do my commendations. I would be glad to see it. |
| BIRON | I would you heard it groan. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Is the [[Fool\|fool]] sick? |
| BIRON | Sick at the heart. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Alack, let it [[Blood\|blood]]. |
| BIRON | Would that do it good? |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | My physic says ‘Ay’. |
| BIRON | Will you prick’t with your eye? |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Non point, with my knife. |
| BIRON | Now God save thy life. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | And yours, from long living. |
| BIRON | I cannot stay thanksgiving. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Dumaine]] |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | (to Boyet)<br>Sir, I pray you a word. What lady is that same? |
| BOYET | The heir of Alençon, Catherine her name. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | A gallant lady. Monsieur, fare you [[Well\|well]]. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Longueville]] |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | (to Boyet)<br>I beseech you a word, what is she in the white? |
| BOYET | A woman sometimes, an you saw her in the light. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Perchance light in the light. I [[Desire\|desire]] her name. |
| BOYET | She hath but one for herself; to [[Desire\|desire]] that were a shame. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Pray you, sir, whose daughter? |
| BOYET | Her mother’s, I have heard. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | God’s blessing on your beard ! |
| BOYET | Good sir, be not offended.<br>She is an heir of Fauconbridge. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Nay, my choler is ended.<br>She is a most sweet lady. |
| BOYET | Not unlike, sir. That may be. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Longueville]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Biron |
| BIRON | What’s her name in the cap? |
| BOYET | [[Rosaline]], by good hap. |
| BIRON | Is she wedded or no? |
| BOYET | To her will, sir, or so. |
| BIRON | O, you are welcome, sir. Adieu. |
| BOYET | Farewell to me, sir, and welcome to you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Biron |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | That last is Biron, the merry madcap lord.<br>Not a word with him but a jest. |
| BOYET | And every jest but a word. |
| PRINCESS | It was [[Well\|well]] done of you to take him at his word. |
| BOYET | I was as willing to grapple as he was to board. |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | Two hot sheeps, marry. |
| BOYET | And wherefore not ships?<br>No sheep, sweet [[Lamb\|lamb]], unless we feed on your lips. |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | You sheep and I pasture—shall that finish the jest? |
| BOYET | So you grant pasture for me. |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | Not so, gentle beast.<br>My lips are no common, though several they be. |
| BOYET | Belonging to whom? |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | To my fortunes and me. |
| PRINCESS | Good wits will be jangling; but, gentles, agree.<br>This civil war of wits were much better used<br>On Navarre and his bookmen, for here ’tis abused. |
| BOYET | If my observation, which very seldom lies,<br>By the heart’s still rhetoric disclosed with eyes,<br>Deceive me not now, Navarre is infected. |
| PRINCESS | With what? |
| BOYET | With that which we [[lovers]] entitle ‘affected’. |
| PRINCESS | Your reason? |
| BOYET | Why, all his behaviours did make their retire<br>To the [[Court\|court]] of his eye, peeping thorough [[Desire\|desire]].<br>His heart like an agate with your print impressed,<br>Proud with his form, in his eye [[Pride\|pride]] expressed.<br>His tongue, all impatient to speak and not see,<br>Did stumble with haste in his eyesight to be.<br>All senses to that sense did make their repair,<br>To feel only looking on fairest of fair.<br>Methought all his senses were locked in his eye,<br>As jewels in crystal, for some prince to buy,<br>Who, tendering their own worth from where they were glassed,<br>Did point you to buy them along as you passed.<br>His face’s own margin did quote such amazes<br>That all eyes saw his eyes enchanted with gazes.<br>I’ll give you Aquitaine and all that is his<br>An you give him for my sake but one loving kiss. |
| PRINCESS | Come, to our pavilion. Boyet is disposed. |
| BOYET | But to speak that in words which his eye hath disclosed.<br>I only have made a mouth of his eye<br>By adding a tongue, which I know will not lie. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] |
| BOYET | Thou art an old [[Love\|love]]-monger, and speak’st skilfully. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Maria\|MARIA]] |
| BOYET | He is Cupid’s grandfather, and learns news of him. |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | Then was [[Venus]] like her mother, for her father is but grim. |
| BOYET | Do you hear, my mad wenches? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[Maria\|MARIA]] |
| BOYET | No. |
| BOYET | What then, do you see? |
| *(didascalia)* | CATHERINE |
| BOYET | Ay—our way to be gone. |
| BOYET | You are too hard for me. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Armado the braggart, and Mote his boy |
| ARMADO | Warble, [[Child\|child]]; make passionate my sense of hearing. |
| MOTE | (sings) Concolinel. |
| ARMADO | Sweet air! Go, tenderness of years, take this key. Give enlargement to the swain. Bring him festinately hither. I must employ him in a letter to my [[Love\|love]]. |
| MOTE | Master, will you win your [[Love\|love]] with a French brawl? |
| ARMADO | How meanest thou—brawling in French? |
| MOTE | No, my complete master; but to jig off a tune at the tongue’s end, canary to it with your feet, [[Humour\|humour]] it with turning up your eyelids, sigh a note and sing a note, sometime through the throat as if you swallowed [[Love\|love]] with singing [[Love\|love]], sometime through the nose as if you snuffed up [[Love\|love]] by smelling [[Love\|love]], with your hat penthouse-like o’er the shop of your eyes, with your arms crossed on your thin-belly doublet like a rabbit on a spit, or your hands in your pocket like a man after the old painting, and keep not too long in one tune, but a snip and away. These are complements, these are humours; these betray nice wenches that would be betrayed without these, and make them men of note—do you note? men—that most are affected to these. |
| ARMADO | How hast thou purchased this experience? |
| MOTE | By my penny of observation. |
| ARMADO | But O, but O- |
| MOTE | ‘The hobby-[[Horse\|horse]] is forgot.’ |
| ARMADO | Call’st thou my [[Love\|love]] hobby-[[Horse\|horse]] ? |
| MOTE | No, master, the hobby-[[Horse\|horse]] is but a colt, and your [[Love\|love]] perhaps a hackney. But have you forgot your [[Love\|love]]? |
| ARMADO | Almost I had. |
| MOTE | Negligent student, learn her by heart. |
| ARMADO | By heart and in heart, boy. |
| MOTE | And out of heart, master. All those three I will prove. 36 |
| ARMADO | What wilt thou prove? |
| MOTE | A man, if I live; and this, ‘by’, ‘in’, and ‘without’, upon the instant: ‘by’ heart you [[Love\|love]] her because your heart cannot come by her; ‘in’ heart you [[Love\|love]] her because your heart is in [[Love\|love]] with her; and ‘out’ of heart you [[Love\|love]] her, being out of heart that you cannot enjoy her. |
| ARMADO | I am all these three. |
| MOTE | (aside) And three times as much more, and yet nothing at all. |
| ARMADO | Fetch hither the swain. He must carry me a letter. |
| MOTE | (aside) A message [[Well\|well]] sympathized—a [[Horse\|horse]] to be ambassador for an ass. |
| ARMADO | Ha, ha! What sayst thou? |
| MOTE | Marry, sir, you must send the ass upon the [[Horse\|horse]], for he is very slow-gaited. But I go. |
| ARMADO | The way is but short. Away! |
| MOTE | As swift as lead, sir. 55 |
| ARMADO | The meaning, pretty ingenious?<br>Is not lead a metal heavy, dull, and slow ? |
| MOTE | Minime, honest master—or rather, master, no. |
| ARMADO | I say lead is slow. |
| MOTE | You are too swift, sir, to say so.<br>Is that lead slow which is fired from a gun? |
| ARMADO | Sweet smoke of rhetoric!<br>He reputes me a cannon, and the bullet, that’s he.<br>I shoot thee at the swain. |
| MOTE | Thump, then, and I flee. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| ARMADO | A most acute juvenal—voluble and free of grace.<br>By thy favour, sweet welkin, I must sigh in thy face.<br>Most rude [[Melancholy\|melancholy]], valour gives thee place.<br>My herald is returned. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Mote the page, and [[Costard]] the clown |
| MOTE | A [[Wonder\|wonder]], master—here’s a [[Costard\|costard]] broken in a shin. |
| ARMADO | Some enigma, some [[Riddle\|riddle]]; come, thy l’envoi. Begin. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | No egma, no [[Riddle\|riddle]], no l‘envoi, no salve in the mail, sir. O sir, plantain, a plain plantain—no l’envoi, no l’envoi, no salve, sir, but a plantain. |
| ARMADO | By virtue, thou enforcest laughter—thy silly thought my spleen. The heaving of my lungs provokes me to ridiculous smiling. O pardon me, my [[Stars\|stars]]! Doth the inconsiderate take salve for l‘envoi, and the word l’envoi for a salve? |
| MOTE | Do the wise think them other? Is not l’envoi a salve ? |
| ARMADO | No, page, it is an epilogue or discourse to make plain<br>Some obscure precedence that hath tofore been sain.<br>I will example it. so<br>The fox, the ape, and the humble-bee<br>Were still at odds, being but three.<br>There’s the moral. Now the /’envoi. |
| MOTE | I will add the l’envoi. Say the moral again. |
| ARMADO | The fox, the ape, and the humble-bee<br>Were still at odds, being but three. |
| MOTE | Until the goose came out of door<br>And stayed the odds by adding four.<br>Now will I begin your moral, and do you follow with my l’envoi.<br>The fox, the ape, and the humble-bee<br>Were still at odds, being but three. |
| ARMADO | Until the goose came out of door,<br>Staying the odds by adding four. |
| MOTE | A good l’envoi, ending in the goose. Would you [[Desire\|desire]] more? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | The boy hath sold him a bargain—a goose, that’s flat.<br>Sir, your pennyworth is good an your goose be fat.<br>To sell a bargain [[Well\|well]] is as cunning as fast and loose.<br>Let me see, a fat l’envoi-ay, that’s a fat goose. |
| ARMADO | Come hither, come hither. How did this argument begin? |
| MOTE | By saying that a [[Costard\|costard]] was broken in a shin.<br>Then called you for the l’envoi. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | True, and I for a plantain. Thus came your argument in. Then the boy’s fat l’envoi, the goose that you bought, and he ended the market. |
| ARMADO | But tell me, how was there a [[Costard\|costard]] broken in a shin? |
| MOTE | I will tell you sensibly. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Thou hast no feeling of it. Mote, I will speak that l’envoi.<br>I, [[Costard]], running out, that was safely within,<br>Fell over the threshold and broke my shin. |
| ARMADO | We will talk no more of this matter. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Till there be more matter in the shin. |
| ARMADO | Sirrah [[Costard]], I will enfranchise thee. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | O, marry me to one Frances! I smell some l’envoi, some goose, in this. |
| ARMADO | By my sweet [[Soul\|soul]], I mean setting thee at liberty, enfreedoming thy person. Thou wert immured, restrained, captivated, bound. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | True, true, and now you will be my purgation and let me loose. |
| ARMADO | I give thee thy liberty, set thee from durance, and in lieu thereof impose on thee nothing but this: bear this significant to the country maid, Jaquenetta. (Giving him a letter) There is remuneration (giving him money), for the best ward of mine [[Honour\|honour]] is rewarding my dependants. Mote, follow. Exit |
| MOTE | Like the sequel, I. Signor [[Costard]], adieu. Exit |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | My sweet ounce of man’s flesh, my incony Jew!<br>Now will I look to his remuneration. Remuneration—<br>O, that’s the Latin word for three-farthings. Three<br>farthings—remuneration. ‘What’s the price of this<br>inkle?’ ‘One penny.’ ‘No, I’ll give you a remuneration.’<br>Why, it carries it! Remuneration! Why, it is a fairer<br>name than French [[Crown\|crown]]. I will never buy and sell out<br>of this word. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Biron |
| BIRON | My good knave [[Costard]], exceedingly [[Well\|well]] met. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Pray you, sir, how much carnation ribbon may a man buy for a remuneration? |
| BIRON | What is a remuneration? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Marry, sir, halfpenny-farthing. |
| BIRON | Why, then, three-farthing-worth of silk. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I thank your worship. God be wi’ you. |
| BIRON | Stay, slave, I must employ thee.<br>As thou wilt win my favour, good my knave,<br>Do one thing for me that I shall entreat. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | When would you have it done, sir? |
| BIRON | This afternoon. |
| CUSTARD | [[Well]], I will do it, sir. Fare you [[Well\|well]]. |
| BIRON | Thou knowest not what it is. |
| CUSTARD | I shall know, sir, when I have done it. |
| BIRON | Why, [[Villain\|villain]], thou must know first. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I will come to your worship tomorrow morning. |
| BIRON | It must be done this afternoon. Hark, slave,<br>It is but this:<br>The Princess comes to hunt here in the park,<br>And in her train there is a gentle lady.<br>When tongues speak sweetly, then they name her<br>name,<br>And [[Rosaline]] they call her. Ask for her,<br>And to her white hand see thou do commend<br>This sealed-up counsel. There’s thy guerdon (giving<br>him a letter and money), go. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Guerdon! O sweet guerdon!—better than remuneration, elevenpence-farthing better—most sweet guerdon! I will do it, sir, in print. Guerdon—remuneration. Exit |
| BIRON | And I, forsooth, in [[Love\|love]]—I that have been [[Love\|love]]’s whip,<br>A very beadle to a humorous sigh,<br>A critic, nay, a [[Night\|night]]-watch constable,<br>A domineering pedant o‘er the boy,<br>Than whom no mortal so magnificent.<br>This wimpled, whining, purblind, wayward boy,<br>This Signor Junior, giant [[Dwarf\|dwarf]], Dan Cupid,<br>Regent of [[Love\|love]]-rhymes, lord of folded arms,<br>Th’anointed sovereign of sighs and groans,<br>Liege of all loiterers and malcontents,<br>Dread prince of plackets, [[King\|king]] of codpieces,<br>Sole imperator and great general<br>Of trotting paritors—O my little heart!<br>And I to be a corporal of his field,<br>And wear his colours like a tumbler’s hoop!<br>What? I [[Love\|love]], I sue, I seek a wife?—<br>A woman, that is like a German [[Clock\|clock]],<br>Still a-repairing, ever out of frame,<br>And never going aright, being a watch,<br>But being watched that it may still go right.<br>Nay, to be perjured, which is worst of all,<br>And among three to [[Love\|love]] the worst of all—<br>A whitely wanton with a velvet brow,<br>With two pitch-balls stuck in her face for eyes—<br>Ay, and, by heaven, one that will do the deed<br>Though Argus were her eunuch and her guard.<br>And I to sigh for her, to watch for her,<br>To pray for her—go to, it is a plague<br>That Cupid will impose for my neglect<br>Of his almighty dreadful little might.<br>[[Well]], I will [[Love\|love]], write, sigh, pray, sue, groan:<br>Some men must [[Love\|love]] my lady, and some Joan. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Princess, a Forester, her ladies-[[Rosaline]], [[Maria]], and Catherine—and her lords, among them Boyet |
| PRINCESS | Was that the [[King]] that spurred his [[Horse\|horse]] so hard<br>Against the steep uprising of the [[Hill\|hill]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | BOYET |
| PRINCESS | I know not, but I think it was not he. |
| PRINCESS | Whoe’er a was, a showed a mounting mind.<br>[[Well]], lords, today we shall have our dispatch.<br>Ere Saturday we will return to France.<br>Then, forester my friend, where is the bush<br>That we must stand and play the murderer in? |
| FORESTER | Hereby, upon the edge of yonder coppice—<br>A stand where you may make the fairest shoot. |
| PRINCESS | I thank my [[Beauty\|beauty]], I am fair that shoot,<br>And thereupon thou speak’st ‘the fairest shoot’. |
| FORESTER | Pardon me, madam, for I meant not so. |
| PRINCESS | What, what? First praise me, and again say no?<br>O short-lived [[Pride\|pride]]! Not fair? Alack, for woe ! |
| FORESTER | Yes, madam, fair. |
| PRINCESS | Nay, never paint me now.<br>Where fair is not, praise cannot mend the brow.<br>Here, good my glass, take this for telling true.<br>She gives him money<br>Fair payment for foul words is more than due. |
| FORESTER | Nothing but fair is that which you inherit. |
| PRINCESS | See, see, my [[Beauty\|beauty]] will be saved by merit!<br>O heresy in fair, fit for these days—<br>A giving hand, though foul, shall have fair praise.<br>But come, the bow. Now [[Mercy\|mercy]] goes to kill,<br>And shooting [[Well\|well]] is then accounted ill.<br>Thus will I save my credit in the shoot,<br>Not wounding—pity would not let me do’t.<br>If wounding, then it was to show my skill,<br>That more for praise than purpose meant to kill.<br>And, out of question, so it is sometimes—<br>Glory grows guilty of detested crimes<br>When for fame’s sake, for praise, an outward part,<br>We bend to that the working of the heart,<br>As I for praise [[Alone\|alone]] now seek to spill<br>The poor deer’s [[Blood\|blood]] that my heart means no ill. |
| BOYET | Do not curst wives hold that [[Self\|self]]-sovereignty<br>Only for praise’ sake when they strive to be<br>Lords o’er their lords? |
| PRINCESS | Only for praise, and praise we may afford<br>To any lady that subdues a lord. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Costard]] the clown |
| BOYET | Here comes a member of the commonwealth. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | God dig-you-de’en, all. Pray you, which is the<br>head lady? |
| PRINCESS | Thou shalt know her, fellow, by the rest that have no heads. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Which is the greatest lady, the highest? |
| PRINCESS | The thickest and the tallest. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | The thickest and the tallest—it is so, [[Truth\|truth]] is [[Truth\|truth]].<br>An your waist, mistress, were as slender as my wit<br>One o’ these maids’ girdles for your waist should be fit.<br>Are not you the chief woman? You are the thickest here. |
| PRINCESS | What’s your will, sir? What’s your will? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I have a letter from Monsieur Biron to one Lady [[Rosaline]]. |
| PRINCESS | O, thy letter, thy letter! (She takes it) He’s a good friend of mine.<br>(To [[Costard]]) Stand aside, good bearer. Boyet, you can carve.<br>Break up this capon.<br>She gives the letter to Boyet |
| BOYET | I am bound to serve.<br>This letter is mistook. It importeth none here.<br>It is writ to Jaquenetta. |
| PRINCESS | We will read it, I swear.<br>Break the neck of the wax, and everyone give ear. |
| BOYET | (reads) ‘By heaven, that thou art fair is most infallible, true that thou art beauteous, [[Truth\|truth]] itself that thou art lovely. More fairer than fair, beautiful than beauteous, truer than [[Truth\|truth]] itself, have commiseration on thy heroical vassal. The magnanimous and most illustrate [[King]] Cophetua set’s eye upon the penurious and indubitate beggar Zenelophon, and he it was that might rightly say “Veni, vidi, vicí”, which to annothanize in the vulgar—O base and obscure vulgar!—videlicet “He came, see, and overcame.” He came, one; see, two; overcame, three. Who came? The [[King]]. Why did he come? To see. Why did he see? To overcome. To whom came he? To the beggar. What saw he? The beggar. Who overcame he? The beggar. The conclusion is victory. On whose side? The [[King]]’s. The captive is enriched. On whose side? The beggar’s. The catastrophe is a nuptial. On whose side? The [[King]]’s—no, on both in one, or one in both. I am the [[King]]—for so stands the comparison—thou the beggar, for so witnesseth thy lowliness. Shall I command thy [[Love\|love]]? I may. Shall I enforce thy [[Love\|love]]? I could. Shall I entreat thy [[Love\|love]]? I will. What shalt thou exchange for rags? Robes. For tittles? Titles. For thyself? Me. Thus, expecting thy reply, I profane my lips on thy foot, my eyes on thy picture, and my heart on thy every part.<br>Thine in the dearest design of industry,<br>Don Adriano de Armado.<br>Thus dost thou hear the Nemean [[Lion\|lion]] roar<br>‘Gainst thee, thou [[Lamb\|lamb]], that standest as his prey.<br>Submissive fall his princely feet before,<br>And he from forage will incline to play.<br>But if thou strive, poor [[Soul\|soul]], what art thou then?<br>Food for his rage, repasture for his den.’ |
| PRINCESS | What plume of feathers is he that indited this letter?<br>What vane? What weathercock? Did you ever hear<br>better? |
| BOYET | I am much deceived but I remember the style. |
| PRINCESS | Else your [[Memory\|memory]] is bad, going o’er it erewhile. |
| BOYET | This Armado is a Spaniard that keeps here in [[Court\|court]],<br>A phantasim, a Monarcho, and one that makes sport<br>To the Prince and his bookmates. |
| PRINCESS | (to [[Costard]]) Thou, fellow, a word.<br>Who gave thee this letter? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | I told you—my lord. |
| PRINCESS | To whom shouldst thou give it? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | From my lord to my lady. |
| PRINCESS | From which lord to which lady? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | From my lord Biron, a good master of mine,<br>To a lady of France that he called [[Rosaline]]. |
| PRINCESS | Thou hast mistaken his letter. Come, lords, away.<br>(To [[Rosaline]], giving her the letter)<br>Here, sweet, put up this, ‘twill be thine another day. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit attended |
| BOYET | Who is the suitor? Who is the suitor? |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Shall I teach you to know? |
| BOYET | Ay, my continent of [[Beauty\|beauty]]. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Why, she that bears the bow.<br>Finely put off. |
| BOYET | My lady goes to kill horns, but if thou marry,<br>Hang me by the neck if horns that year miscarry.<br>Finely put on. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | [[Well]] then, I am the shooter. |
| BOYET | And who is your deer? |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | If we choose by the horns, yourself come not near.<br>Finely put on indeed! |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | You still wrangle with her, Boyet, and she strikes at the brow. |
| BOYET | But she herself is hit tower—have I hit her now? |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | Shall I come upon thee with an old saying that was a man when [[King]] Pépin of France was a little boy, as touching the hit it? |
| BOYET | So I may answer thee with one as old that was a woman when [[Queen]] Guinevere of Britain was a little wench, as touching the hit it. |
| [[Rosaline\|ROSALINE]] | (sings)<br>Thou canst not hit it, hit it, hit it,<br>Thou canst not hit it, my good man. |
| BOYET | (sings)<br>An I cannot, cannot, cannot,<br>An I cannot, another can. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[Rosaline]] |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | By my troth, most pleasant How both did fit it! |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | A mark marvellous [[Well\|well]] shot, for they both did hit it. |
| BOYET | A mark—O mark but that mark! A mark, says my<br>lady.<br>Let the mark have a prick in’t to mete at, if it may be. |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | Wide o’ the bow hand—i’[[Faith\|faith]], your hand is out. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Indeed, a must shoot nearer, or he’ll ne’er hit the clout. |
| BOYET | An if my hand be out, then belike your hand is in. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Then will she get the upshoot by cleaving the pin. |
| [[Maria\|MARIA]] | Come, come, you talk greasily, your lips grow foul. |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | She’s too hard for you at pricks, sir. Challenge her to bowl. |
| BOYET | I [[Fear\|fear]] too much rubbing. Goodnight, my good owl. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Boyet, [[Maria]], and Catherine |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | By my [[Soul\|soul]], a swain, a most simple clown.<br>Lord, Lord, how the ladies and I have put him down!<br>O’ my troth, most sweet jests, most incony vulgar wit,<br>When it comes so smoothly off, so obscenely, as it were, so fit!<br>Armado o‘th’ t’other side—O, a most dainty man!—<br>To see him walk before a lady and to bear her fan!<br>To see him kiss his hand, and how most sweetly a will swear,<br>And his page o’ t’other side, that handful of wit—<br>Ah heavens, it is a most pathetical nit! |
| *(didascalia)* | Shout within |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Sola, sola! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Dull, Holofernes the pedant, and Nathaniel the curate |
| NATHANIEL | Very reverend sport, truly, and done in the testimony of a good [[Conscience\|conscience]]. |
| HOLOFERNES | The deer was, as you know—sanguis—in [[Blood\|blood]], ripe as the pomewater who now hangeth like a [[Jewel\|jewel]] in the ear of caelo, the sky, the welkin, the heaven, and anon falleth like a crab on the face of terra, the soil, the land, the earth. |
| NATHANIEL | Truly, Master Holofernes, the epithets are sweetly varied, like a scholar at the least. But, sir, I assure ye it was a buck of the first head. |
| HOLOFERNES | Sir Nathaniel, haud credo. |
| DULL | ‘Twas not a ‘auld grey doe’, ’twas a pricket. |
| HOLOFERNES | Most barbarous intimation! Yet a kind of insinuation, as it were in via, in way, of explication, facere, as it were, replication, or rather ostentare, to show, as it were, his inclination after his undressed, unpolished, uneducated, unpruned, untrained, or rather unlettered, or ratherest unconfirmed, fashion, to insert again my ‘haud credo’ for a deer. |
| DULL | I said the deer was not a ‘auld grey doe’, ‘twas a pricket. |
| HOLOFERNES | Twice-sod simplicity, bis coctus!<br>O thou [[Monster\|monster]] ignorance, how deformed dost thou look! |
| NATHANIEL | Sir, he hath never fed of the dainties that are bred in<br>a book.<br>He hath not eat paper, as it were, he hath not drunk<br>ink. His intellect is not replenished, he is only an<br>animal, only sensible in the duller parts,<br>And such barren plants are set before us that we<br>thankful should be,<br>Which we of taste and feeling are, for those parts that<br>do fructify in us more than he.<br>For as it would ill become me to be vain, indiscreet,<br>or a [[Fool\|fool]],<br>So were there a patch set on learning to see him in a<br>school.<br>But omne bene say I, being of an old father’s mind:<br>‘Many can brook the weather that [[Love\|love]] not the [[Wind\|wind]].’ |
| DULL | You two are bookmen. Can you tell me by your wit<br>What was a month old at [[Cain]]’s birth that’s not five weeks old as yet? |
| HOLOFERNES | Dictynna, Goodman Dull, Dictynna, Goodman Dull. |
| DULL | What is ‘Dictima’? |
| NATHANIEL | A title to [[Phoebe]], to luna, to the [[Moon\|moon]]. |
| HOLOFERNES | The [[Moon\|moon]] was a month old when Adam was no more,<br>And raught not to five weeks when he came to five score.<br>Th’allusion holds in the exchange. |
| DULL | ’Tis true, indeed, the collusion holds in the exchange. |
| HOLOFERNES | God comfort thy capacity, I say th’allusion holds in the exchange. |
| DULL | And I say the pollution holds in the exchange, for the [[Moon\|moon]] is never but a month old—and I say beside that ’twas a pricket that the Princess killed. |
| HOLOFERNES | Sir Nathaniel, will you hear an extemporal epitaph on the [[Death\|death]] of the deer? And to [[Humour\|humour]] the ignorant call I the deer the Princess killed a pricket. |
| NATHANIEL | Perge, good Master Holofernes, perge, so it shall please you to abrogate scurrility. |
| HOLOFERNES | I will something affect the letter, for it argues facility.<br>The preyful Princess pierced and pricked a pretty pleasing pricket.<br>Some say a sore, but not a sore till now made sore with shooting.<br>The [[dogs]] did yell; put ‘I’ to ‘sore‘, then ‘sorel’ jumps from thicket—<br>Or pricket sore, or else sorel. The people fall a- hooting.<br>If sore be sore, then ‘I’ to ‘sore’ makes fifty sores—O sore ‘I’!<br>Of one sore I an hundred make by adding but one more ‘I’. |
| NATHANIEL | A rare talent! |
| DULL | If a talent be a claw, look how he claws him with a talent. |
| HOLOFERNES | This is a gift that I have, simple, simpte—a foolish extravagant spirit, full of forms, figures, shapes, objects, ideas, apprehensions, motions, revolutions. These are begot in the ventricle of [[Memory\|memory]], nourished in the womb of pia mater, and delivered upon the mellowing of occasion. But the gift is good in those in whom it is acute, and I am thankful for it. |
| NATHANIEL | Sir, I praise the Lord for you, and so may my parishioners; for their sons are [[Well\|well]] tutored by you, and their daughters profit very greatly under you. You are a good member of the commonwealth. |
| HOLOFERNES | Mehercle, if their sons be ingenious they shall want no instruction; if their daughters be capable, I will put it to them. But Vir sapit qui pauca loquitur; a [[Soul\|soul]] feminine saluteth us. Enter Jaquenetta, and [[Costard]] the clown |
| JAQUENETTA | God give you good-morrow, Master Parson. |
| HOLOFERNES | Master Parson, quasi ‘pierce one’ ? And if one should be pierced, which is the one? |
| [[Costard\|COSTARD]] | Marry, Master Schoolmaster, he that is likeliest to a hogshead. |
| HOLOFERNES | ‘Of piercing a hogshead’—a good lustre of conceit in a turf of earth, [[Fire\|fire]] enough for a flint, pearl enough for a swine—‘tis pretty, it is [[Well\|well]]. |
| JAQUENETTA | Good Master Parson, be so good as read me this letter. It was given me by [[Costard]], and sent me from [[Don Armado]]. I beseech you read it.<br>She gives the letter to Nathaniel, who reads it |
| HOLOFERNES | (to himself) ‘Facile precor gelida quando pecas omnia sub umbra ruminat’, and so forth. Ah, good old Mantuan! I may speak of thee as the traveller doth of [[Venice]]:<br>Venezia, Venezia, Chi non ti vede, chi non ti prezia.<br>Old Mantuan, old Mantuan—who understandeth thee not, loves thee not. (He sings) Ut, re, Sol, la, mi, fa. (To Nathaniel) Under pardon, sir, what are the contents? Or rather, as Horace says in his—what, my [[Soul\|soul]]—verses? |
| NATHANIEL | Ay, sir, and very learned. |
| HOLOFERNES | Let me hear a staff, a stanza, a verse. Lege, domine. |
| NATHANIEL | (reads)<br>‘If [[Love\|love]] make me forsworn, how shall I swear to [[Love\|love]]?<br>Ah, never [[Faith\|faith]] could hold, if not to [[Beauty\|beauty]] vowed.<br>Though to myself forsworn, to thee I’ll faithful prove.<br>Those thoughts to me were oaks, to thee like osiers bowed.<br>Study his bias [[Leaves\|leaves]], and makes his book thine eyes,<br>Where all those pleasures live that art would comprehend.<br>If knowledge be the mark, to know thee shall suffice.<br>[[Well]] learned is that tongue that [[Well\|well]] can thee commend;<br>All ignorant that [[Soul\|soul]] that sees thee without [[Wonder\|wonder]];<br>Which is to me some praise that I thy parts admire.<br>Thy eye Jove’s [[Lightning\|lightning]] bears, thy voice his dreadful thunder,<br>Which, not to anger bent, is music and sweet [[Fire\|fire]].<br>Celestial as thou art, O pardon, [[Love\|love]], this wrong,<br>That singeth heaven’s praise with such an earthly tongue.’ |
| HOLOFERNES | You find not the apostrophus, and so miss the accent. Let me supervise the canzonet. Here are only numbers ratified, but for the elegancy, facility, and golden cadence of poesy—caret. Ovidius Naso was the man. And why indeed ‘Naso’ but for smelling out the odoriferous [[Flowers\|flowers]] of fancy, the jerks of invention? Imitari is nothing. So doth the hound his master, the ape his keeper, the tired [[Horse\|horse]] his rider. But domicella— virgin—was this directed to you? |
| JAQUENETTA | Ay, Sir. |
| HOLOFERNES | I will overglance the superscript. ‘To the [[Snow\|snow]]-white hand of the most beauteous Lady [[Rosaline]].’ I will look again on the intellect of the letter for the nomination of the party writing to the person written unto. ‘Your ladyship’s in all desired employment, Biron.’ Sir Nathaniel, this Biron is one of the votaries with the [[King]], and here he hath framed a letter to a sequent of the stranger [[Queen]]’s, which, accidentally or by the way of progression, hath miscarried. (To Jaquenetta) Trip and go, my sweet, deliver this paper into the royal hand of the [[King]]. It may concern much. Stay not thy compliment, I forgive thy duty. Adieu. |
| JAQUENETTA | Good [[Costard]], go with me.—Sir, God save your life. |
| CUSTARD | Have with thee, my girl. Exit with Jaquenetta |
| NATHANIEL | Sir, you have done this in the [[Fear\|fear]] of God very religiously, and, as a certain father saith— |
| HOLOFERNES | Sir, tell not me of the father; I do [[Fear\|fear]] colourable colours. But to return to the verses—did they please you, Sir Nathaniel? |
| NATHANIEL | Marvellous [[Well\|well]] for the pen. |
| HOLOFERNES | I do dine today at the father’s of a certain pupil of mine where, if before repast it shall please you to gratify the table with a grace, I will on my privilege I have with the parents of the foresaid [[Child\|child]] or pupil undertake your ben venuto, where I will prove those verses to be very unlearned, neither savouring of poetry, wit, nor invention. I beseech your society. |
| NATHANIEL | And thank you too, for society, saith the text, is the happiness of life. |
| HOLOFERNES | And certes the text most infallibly concludes it. (To Dull) Sir, I do invite you too. You shall not say me nay. Pauca verba. Away, the gentles are at their game, and we will to our recreation. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Biron with a paper in his hand, [[Alone\|alone]] |
| BIRON | The [[King]], he is hunting the deer. I am coursing myself. They have pitched a toil, I am toiling in a pitch—pitch that defiles. Denle—a foul word. [[Well]], set thee down, sorrow; for so they say the [[Fool\|fool]] said, and so say I, and I the [[Fool\|fool]]. [[Well]] proved, wit! By the Lord, this [[Love\|love]] is as mad as [[Ajax]], it kills sheep, it kills me, I a sheep—[[Well\|well]] proved again o’ my side. I will not [[Love\|love]]. If I do, hang me; i’[[Faith\|faith]], I will not. O, but her eye! By this light, but for her eye I would not [[Love\|love]] her. Yes, for her two eyes. [[Well]], I do nothing in the world but lie, and lie in my throat. By heaven, I do [[Love\|love]], and it hath taught me to rhyme and to be [[Melancholy\|melancholy]], and here (showing a paper) is part of my rhyme, and here (touching his breast) my [[Melancholy\|melancholy]]. [[Well]], she hath one o’ my sonnets already. The clown bore it, the [[Fool\|fool]] sent it, and the lady hath it. Sweet clown, sweeter [[Fool\|fool]], sweetest lady. By the world, I would not care a pin if the other three were in. Here comes one with a paper. God give him grace to groan.<br>He stands aside. The [[King]] entereth with a paper |
| [[King\|KING]] | Ay me! |
| BIRON | (aside) Shot, by heaven! Proceed, sweet Cupid, thou hast thumped him with thy birdbolt under the left pap. In [[Faith\|faith]], [[Secrets\|secrets]]. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (reads)<br>‘So sweet a kiss the golden sun gives not<br>To those fresh morning drops upon the [[Rose\|rose]]<br>As thy eyebeams when their fresh rays have smote<br>The [[Night\|night]] of dew that on my cheeks down flows.<br>Nor shines the silver [[Moon\|moon]] one-half so bright<br>Through the transparent bosom of the deep<br>As doth thy face through [[Tears\|tears]] of mine give light.<br>Thou shin’st in every tear that I do weep.<br>No drop but as a coach doth carry thee,<br>So ridest thou triumphing in my woe.<br>Do but behold the [[Tears\|tears]] that swell in me<br>And they thy glory through my grief will show.<br>But do not [[Love\|love]] thyself; then thou wilt keep<br>My [[Tears\|tears]] for glasses, and still make me weep.<br>O [[Queen]] of queens, how far dost thou excel,<br>No thought can think nor tongue of mortal tell.’<br>How shall she know my griefs? I’ll drop the paper.<br>Sweet [[Leaves\|leaves]], shade folly. Who is he comes here? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Longueville]] with papers. The [[King]] steps aside |
| [[King\|KING]] | What, [[Longueville]], and reading—listen, ear! |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Now in thy likeness one more [[Fool\|fool]] appear! |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Ay me! I am forsworn. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Why, he comes in like a perjure, wearing papers. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (aside)<br>In [[Love\|love]], I [[Hope\|hope]]! Sweet fellowship in shame. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>One drunkard loves another of the name. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | Am I the first that have been perjured so? |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>I could put thee in comfort, not by two that I know.<br>Thou makest the triumviry, the corner-cap of society,<br>The shape of [[Love\|love]]’s Tyburn, that hangs up simplicity. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | I [[Fear\|fear]] these stubborn lines lack power to move.<br>O sweet [[Maria]], empress of my [[Love\|love]],<br>These numbers will I tear, and write in prose. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>O, rhymes are guards on wanton Cupid’s hose,<br>Disfigure not his slop. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | This same shall go.<br>He reads the [[Sonnet\|sonnet]]<br>‘Did not the heavenly rhetoric of thine eye,<br>’Gainst whom the world cannot hold argument,<br>Persuade my heart to this false perjury?<br>Vows for thee broke deserve not punishment.<br>A woman I forswore, but I will prove,<br>Thou being a goddess, I forswore not thee.<br>My vow was earthly, thou a heavenly [[Love\|love]].<br>Thy grace being gained cures all disgrace in me.<br>Vows are but breath, and breath a vapour is.<br>Then thou, fair sun, which on my earth dost shine,<br>Exhal‘st this vapour-vow; in thee it is.<br>If broken then, it is no fault of mine.<br>If by me broke, what [[Fool\|fool]] is not so wise<br>To lose an oath to win a paradise?’ |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>This is the liver vein, which makes flesh a deity,<br>A green goose a goddess, pure, pure idolatry.<br>God amend us, God amend: we are much out o’th’<br>way. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[Dumaine]] with a paper |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | (aside)<br>By whom shall I send this? Company? Stay.<br>He steps aside |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>All hid, all hid—an old infant play.<br>Like a demigod here sit I in the sky,<br>And wretched fools’ [[Secrets\|secrets]] heedfully o’er-eye.<br>More sacks to the mill! O heavens, I have my wish.<br>[[Dumaine]] transformed—four woodcocks in a dish! |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | O most divine Kate! |
| BIRON | (aside) O most profane coxcomb! |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | By heaven, the [[Wonder\|wonder]] in a mortal eye! |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>By earth, she is not, corporal; there you lie. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | Her amber hairs for foul hath amber quoted. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>An amber-coloured raven was [[Well\|well]] noted. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | As upright as the cedar. |
| BIRON | (aside) Stoop, I say.<br>Her shoulder is with [[Child\|child]]. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | As fair as day. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Ay, as some days; but then no sun must shine. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | O that I had my wishl |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | (aside) And I had mine! |
| [[King\|KING]] | (aside) And I mine too, good Lord! |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Amen, so I had mine. Is not that a good word? |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | I would forget her, but a fever she<br>Reigns in my [[Blood\|blood]] and will remembered be. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>A fever in your [[Blood\|blood]]—why then, incision<br>Would let her out in saucers—sweet misprision. |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | Once more I’ll read the ode that I have writ. |
| BIRON | (aside)<br>Once more I’ll mark how [[Love\|love]] can vary wit.<br>[[Dumaine]] reads his [[Sonnet\|sonnet]] |
| [[Dumaine\|DUMAINE]] | ‘On a day—atack the day—<br>[[Love]], whose month is ever May,<br>Spied a blossom passing fair<br>Playing in the wanton air.<br>Through the velvet [[Leaves\|leaves]] the [[Wind\|wind]]<br>All unseen can passage find,<br>That the [[Lover\|lover]], sick to [[Death\|death]],<br>Wished himself the heavens’ breath.<br>“Air”, quoth he, “thy cheeks may blow;<br>Air, would I might triumph so.<br>But, alack, my hand is sworn<br>Ne’er to pluck thee from thy thorn—<br>Vow, alack, for youth unmeet,<br>Youth so apt to pluck a sweet.<br>Do not call it sin in me<br>That I am forsworn for thee,<br>Thou for whom great Jove would swear<br>Juno but an Ethiop were,<br>And deny himself for Jove,<br>Turning mortal for thy [[Love\|love]].”’<br>This will I send, and something else more plain,<br>That shall express my true [[Love\|love]]’s fasting [[Pain\|pain]].<br>O, would the [[King]], Biron, and [[Longueville]]<br>Were [[lovers]] too! Ill to example ill<br>Would from my forehead wipe a perjured note,<br>For none offend where all alike do dote. |
| [[Longueville\|LONGUEVILLE]] | (coming forward)<br>[[Dumaine]], thy [[Love\|love]] is far from [[Charity\|charity]],<br>That in [[Love\|love]]’s grief desir‘st society.<br>You may look pale, but I should blush, I know,<br>To be o’erheard and taken napping so. |
| [[King\|KING]] | (coming forward)<br>Come, sir, you blush. As his, your case is such.<br>You chide at him, offending twice as much.<br>You do not [[Love\|love]] [[Maria]]?
